together  in  fuch  wife  that  if  others  heard  it  they  would 
declare  them  mad  or  drunk  ? 

Now,  dear  children,  I  fear  that  I  have  kept  you  too 

long;  but  the  time  has  not  feemed 
long  to  me :  alfo,  I  have  faid  it  all 
for  your  good,  and  could  not  well  this  time  make  my 
iermon  fliorter  if  I  were  rightly  to  explain  my  meaning ; 
therefore  receive  it  kindly. 

That  we  may  all  become  real,  true,  perfeft  brides  of  our 
Lord  Jefus  Chrift,  and  that  we  may  in  fincere,  true,  utter 
humility  and  refignation,  go  out  to  meet  our  glorious

RESPECTING 

TAULER'S  LIFE  AND  TIMES. 
By  the  Translator. 


^OHN  TAULER,  who  appears  **as  the 
Mafter"  in  the  foregoing  Hiftory,  was  bom 
at  Stialburg  in  the  year  1290.  His  lather 
was  moft  probably  Nicolas  Tauler,  whofe 
name  occurs  among  thofe  of  the  fenators  of  Strafburg  in 
1313.  At  all  events,  be  belonged  to  a  tolerably  wealthy 
£unily,  and  nu^t  have  lived  on  his  patrimony,  fince  he 
tells  us  in  one  of  his  femions:  "Had  I  known  when  I 
lived  as  my  Cither's  fon,  all  that  I  know  now,  I  would 
have  lived  on  his  heritage  and  not  upon  alms."  He 
devoted  himfelfl  however,  in  early  years  to  a  clerical  life, 
and  entered  the  Dominican  Order  in  Strafburg,  taking  up 


76  Tauler's  Life  and  Times. 

his  abode  in  the  handfome,  fpacious  convent  belonging  to 
that  Order,  the  church  of  which  was  confecratcd  in  the 
year  1 308.  A  filler  of  his  was  a  nun  in  the  convent  of 
St  Nicolas  at  Krautenau,  likewife  belonging  to  the 
Dominican  Order.  In  what  year  Tauler  renounced  the 
world  cannot  be  determined  with  precifion,  but  there  can 
be  little  doubt  that  he  did  fo  at  the  fame  time  with  his 
firiend  John  von  Dambach,  in  1308.  From  allufions  in 
his  writings,  it  feems  probable  that  he  foon  after,  with  the 
fame  fi-iend,  betook  himfelf  to  Paris,  the  great  metropolis 
of  Chriftian  learning  in  that  age,  in  order  to  ftudy  theology 
in  the  femous  Dominican  College  of  St  Jacques,  from 
which  the  monks  of  that  Order  were  called  Jacobins  in 
France. 

The  Univerfity  concentrated  within  its  precin&s  repre- 
fentatives  of  the  varied  intellectual  tendencies  of  the  age. 
Up  to  the  middle  of  the  thirteenth  century,  it  had  been 
diftinguiflied  by  the  freedom  of  thought  which  prevailed 
among  its  teachers,  unfliackled  as  they  were  by  any 
episcopal,  almoft  by  any  regal  jurifdidion  over  their 
doSrine,  and  acknowledging  only  the  authority  of  the 
Pope  himfelf,  direftly  exercifed.  The  influence  of  the 
all-queftioning  Abelard,  the  fubtle  Gilbert  de  la  Poree, 
the  pantheiftic  Amaury  de  Bene,  and  other  free-thinking 
teachers,  was  not  extinS,  though  they  lay  under  the  cen- 
fure  of  herefy.    The  wotVls  oi  Kt&ox\^^  coxv^^to^rA.  *\sv

1209,  had  been  gradually  introduced  into  the  fchools,  with 
die  Arabian  commentaries  of  Avicenna  and  Averrhoes. 
The  Dominican  Order,  founded  for  die  extirpation  of 
herefy,  early  recOgnifed  the  prime  neceflity  of  providing 
inftruftion  which  (hould  purify  the  ftreams  of  human 
thought  at  their  fountain-head ;  and  in  fpite  of  the  oppofi- 
tion  raifed  by  the  heads  of  the  Univerfity,  fucceeded,  in 
1 228,  in  eftablifhing  theological  chairs  in  their  convent  in
